Title: De Graecae Ecclesiae hodierno statu epistola (Thomas Smith's Latin letter on the present state of the Greek Church; third edition, 'Editio nova, auctior et emendatior')
Creator: Thomas Smith (1638-1710), Doctor of Divinity and presbyter of the Church of England, formerly chaplain to the English embassy at Constantinople
Publisher: Trajecti ad Rhenum (Utrecht): apud Franciscum Halmam, academiae typographum
Publication Date: 1698
Rights: Public Domain (printed 1698)
Language: lat
Source: Internet Archive item degraecaeecclesi00smit, file degraecaeecclesi00smit_djvu.txt (Getty Research Institute copy), fetched directly by the library/source-research thread (Era VII session, one-world agent for Orthodoxy under the Ottomans), 2026-10-16; host rights statement: none given in the item record (no possible-copyright-status field); the rights basis is the 1698 imprint
Provenance note: Header prepended; the OCR text follows unchanged (the OCR separates words with double spaces and has no long s to normalise; zero replacements). Rights basis restated: a book printed at Utrecht in 1698 is public domain by date.
Content note: The Latin original behind the English 'Account of the Greek Church' of 1680 (vendored as smith-thomas_account-of-the-greek-church_1680.txt); the item record says it was 'previously published 1676 (Oxford) and 1678 (London)', so this is the third, enlarged edition; the 1680 English itself refers to 'the Latine Edition' (about line 1922 of that file) without naming which, so its wording may differ from this text. Order in the file: the Getty scan stub, title page, publisher's address to the reader ('Lectori S.', about line 20), Smith's letter from Oxford dated 6 July (about line 706), the 'Epistola' from about line 722, then 'Index rerum et verborum' (line 7720). The item record says it is bound with Smith's 'Septem Asiae ecclesiarum et Constantinopoleos notitia' (Utrecht, 1694); that tract is not in the OCR. OCR sampled at five points: readable Latin with the usual f-for-long-s substitutions in running text, Greek words garbled. An outside Anglican voice describing the Greek Church, not the church's own voice. Second witness: check quotations against the page images.
